[Narrator] THE CROWNING VICTORY CHAPTER I. INTRODUCES MR. LEWISHAM. [/]
[Narrator] The opening chapter does not concern itself with Love -- indeed that antagonist does not certainly appear until the third -- and Mr. Lewisham is seen at his studies. [/]
[Narrator] It was ten years ago, and in those days he was assistant master in the Whortley Proprietary School, Whortley, Sussex, and his wages were forty pounds a year, out of which he had to afford fifteen shillings a week during term time to lodge with Mrs. Munday, at the little shop in the West Street. [/]
[Lewisham] He was called"Mr."to distinguish him from the bigger boys, whose duty it was to learn, and it was a matter of stringent regulation that he should be addressed as"Sir." [/]
[Narrator] He wore ready - made clothes, his black jacket of rigid line was dusted about the front and sleeves with scholastic chalk, and his face was downy and his moustache incipient. [/]
[Narrator] He was a passable - looking youngster of eighteen, fair - haired, indifferently barbered, and with a quite unnecessary pair of glasses on his fairly prominent nose -- he wore these to make himself look older, that discipline might be maintained. [/]
[Narrator] At the particular moment when this story begins he was in his bedroom. [/]
[Narrator] An attic it was, with lead - framed dormer windows, a slanting ceiling and a bulging wall, covered, as a number of torn places witnessed, with innumerable strata of florid old - fashioned paper. [/]
[Narrator] To judge by the room Mr. Lewisham thought little of Love [/]
[Narrator] but much on Greatness. [/]
[Man] Over the head of the bed, for example, where good folks hang texts, these truths asserted themselves, written in a clear, bold, youthfully florid hand: --"Knowledge is Power,"and"What man has done man can do,"--man in the second instance referring to Mr. Lewisham. [/]
[Narrator] Never for a moment were these things to be forgotten. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Lewisham could see them afresh every morning as his head came through his shirt. [/]
[Lewisham] And over the yellow - painted box upon which -- for lack of shelves -- Mr. Lewisham's library was arranged, was a"_ Schema _." [/]
[Lewisham] (Why he should not have headed it"Scheme,"the editor of the _ Church Times _, who calls his miscellaneous notes"_ Varia _,"is better able to say than I.) [/]
[Lewisham] In this scheme, 1892 was indicated as the year in which Mr. Lewisham proposed to take his B. A. degree at the London University with"hons. [/]
[Lewisham] in all subjects,"and 1895 as the date of his"gold medal." [/]
[Lewisham] Subsequently there were to be"pamphlets in the Liberal interest,"and such like things duly dated. [/]
[Lewisham] "Who would control others must first control himself,"remarked the wall over the wash - hand stand, and behind the door against the Sunday trousers was a portrait of Carlyle. [/]
[Narrator] These were no mere threats against the universe; operations had begun. [/]
[Narrator] Jostling Shakespeare, Emerson's Essays, and the penny Life of Confucius, there were battered and defaced school books, a number of the excellent manuals of the Universal Correspondence Association, exercise books, ink (red and black) in penny bottles, and an india - rubber stamp with Mr. Lewisham's name. [/]
[Narrator] A trophy of bluish green South Kensington certificates for geometrical drawing, astronomy, physiology, physiography, and inorganic chemistry adorned his further wall. [/]
[Narrator] And against the Carlyle portrait was a manuscript list of French irregular verbs. [/]
[Narrator] Attached by a drawing - pin to the roof over the wash - hand stand, which -- the room being an attic -- sloped almost dangerously, dangled a Time - Table. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Lewisham was to rise at five, and that this was no vain boasting, a cheap American alarum clock by the books on the box witnessed. [/]
[Narrator] The lumps of mellow chocolate on the papered ledge by the bed - head indorsed that evidence. [/]
[Lewisham] "French until eight,"said the time - table curtly. [/]
[Lewisham] Breakfast was to be eaten in twenty minutes; then twenty - five minutes of"literature"to be precise, learning extracts (preferably pompous) from the plays of William Shakespeare -- and then to school and duty. [/]
[Lewisham] The time - table further prescribed Latin Composition for the recess and the dinner hour ("literature,"however, during the meal), and varied its injunctions for the rest of the twenty - four hours according to the day of the week. [/]
[Satan] Not a moment for Satan and that"mischief still"of his. [/]
[Narrator] Only three - score and ten has the confidence, as well as the time, to be idle. [/]
[Narrator] But just think of the admirable quality of such a scheme! [/]
[Narrator] Up and busy at five, with all the world about one horizontal, warm, dreamy - brained or stupidly hullish, if roused, roused only to grunt and sigh and roll over again into oblivion. [/]
[Narrator] By eight three hours'clear start, three hours'knowledge ahead of everyone. [/]
[Narrator] It takes, I have been told by an eminent scholar, about a thousand hours of sincere work to learn a language completely -- after three or four languages much less -- which gives you, even at the outset, one each a year before breakfast. [/]
[Narrator] The gift of tongues -- picked up like mushrooms! [/]
[Lewisham] Then that"literature"--an astonishing conception! [/]
[Lewisham] In the afternoon mathematics and the sciences. [/]
[Lewisham] Could anything be simpler or more magnificent? [/]
[Lewisham] In six years Mr. Lewisham will have his five or six languages, a sound, all - round education, a habit of tremendous industry, and be still but four - and - twenty. [/]
[Lewisham] He will already have honour in his university and ampler means. [/]
[Lewisham] One realises that those pamphlets in the Liberal interests will be no obscure platitudes. [/]
[Lewisham] Where Mr. Lewisham will be at thirty stirs the imagination. [/]
[Lewisham] There will be modifications of the Schema, of course, as experience widens. [/]
[Lewisham] But the spirit of it -- the spirit of it is a devouring flame! [/]
[Narrator] He was sitting facing the diamond - framed window, writing, writing fast, on a second yellow box that was turned on end and empty, and the lid was open, and his knees were conveniently stuck into the cavity. [/]
[Narrator] The bed was strewn with books and copygraphed sheets of instructions from his remote correspondence tutors. [/]
[Narrator] Pursuant to the dangling time - table he was, you would have noticed, translating Latin into English. [/]
[Narrator] Imperceptibly the speed of his writing diminished. [/]
[Lewisham] "_ Urit me Glycerae nitor _"lay ahead and troubled him. [/]
[Lewisham] "Urit me,"he murmured, and his eyes travelled from his book out of window to the vicar's roof opposite and its ivied chimneys. [/]
[Narrator] His brows were knit at first and then relaxed. [/]
[Lewisham] "_ Urit me _!" [/]
[Narrator] He had put his pen into his mouth and glanced about for his dictionary. [/]
[Narrator] _ [/]
[Narrator] Urare _? [/]
[Narrator] Suddenly his expression changed. [/]
[Narrator] Movement dictionary - ward ceased. [/]
[Narrator] He was listening to a light tapping sound -- it was a footfall -- outside. [/]
[Narrator] He stood up abruptly, and, stretching his neck, peered through his unnecessary glasses and the diamond panes down into the street. [/]
[Narrator] Looking acutely downward he could see a hat daintily trimmed with pinkish white blossom, the shoulder of a jacket, and just the tips of nose and chin. [/]
[Narrator] Certainly the stranger who sat under the gallery last Sunday next the Frobishers. [/]
[Narrator] Then, too, he had seen her only obliquely. .. . [/]
[Narrator] He watched her until she passed beyond the window frame. [/]
[Narrator] He strained to see impossibly round the corner. .. . [/]

[Lewisham] "This wandering attention!" [/]

[Lewisham] "Urit me,"he said, biting the end of his pen and looking for his dictionary. [/]
[Narrator] It was a Wednesday half - holiday late in March, a spring day glorious in amber light, dazzling white clouds and the intensest blue, casting a powder of wonderful green hither and thither among the trees and rousing all the birds to tumultuous rejoicings, a rousing day, a clamatory insistent day, a veritable herald of summer. [/]
[Narrator] The stir of that anticipation was in the air, the warm earth was parting above the swelling seeds, and all the pine - woods were full of the minute crepitation of opening bud scales. [/]
[Narrator] And not only was the stir of Mother Nature's awakening in the earth and the air and the trees, but also in Mr. Lewisham's youthful blood, bidding him rouse himself to live -- live in a sense quite other than that the Schema indicated. [/]
[Lewisham] He saw the dictionary peeping from under a paper, looked up"Urit me,"appreciated the shining"nitor"of Glycera's shoulders, and so fell idle again to rouse himself abruptly. [/]
[Lewisham] "I _ ca n't _ fix my attention,"said Mr. Lewisham. [/]
[Narrator] He took off the needless glasses, wiped them, and blinked his eyes. [/]
[Narrator] This confounded Horace and his stimulating epithets! [/]
[Narrator] A walk? [/]
[Lewisham] "I wo n't be beat,"he said -- incorrectly -- replaced his glasses, brought his elbows down on either side of his box with resonant violence, and clutched the hair over his ears with both hands. .. . [/]
[Narrator] In five minutes'time he found himself watching the swallows curving through the blue over the vicarage garden. [/]
[Lewisham] "Did ever man have such a bother with himself as me?"he asked vaguely but vehemently. [/]
[Lewisham] "It's self - indulgence does it -- sitting down's the beginning of laziness. [/]
[Lewisham] "So he stood up to his work, and came into permanent view of the village street. [/]
[Lewisham] "If she has gone round the corner by the post office, she will come in sight over the palings above the allotments,"suggested the unexplored and undisciplined region of Mr. Lewisham's mind. .. . [/]
[Narrator] She did not come into sight. [/]
[Narrator] Apparently she had not gone round by the post office after all. [/]
[Narrator] It made one wonder where she had gone. [/]
[Narrator] Did she go up through the town to the avenue on these occasions? .. . [/]
[Narrator] Then abruptly a cloud drove across the sunlight, the glowing street went cold and Mr. Lewisham's imagination submitted to control. [/]
[Lewisham] So"_ Mater saeva cupidinum _,""The untamable mother of desires,"--Horace (Book II. of the Odes) was the author appointed by the university for Mr. Lewisham's matriculation -- was, after all, translated to its prophetic end. [/]
[Narrator] Precisely as the church clock struck five Mr. Lewisham, with a punctuality that was indeed almost too prompt for a really earnest student, shut his Horace, took up his Shakespeare, and descended the narrow, curved, uncarpeted staircase that led from his garret to the living room in which he had his tea with his landlady, Mrs. Munday. [/]
[Narrator] That good lady was alone, and after a few civilities Mr. Lewisham opened his Shakespeare and read from a mark onward -- that mark, by - the - bye, was in the middle of a scene -- while he consumed mechanically a number of slices of bread and whort jam. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Munday watched him over her spectacles and thought how bad so much reading must be for the eyes, until the tinkling of her shop - bell called her away to a customer. [/]
[Lewisham] At twenty - five minutes to six he put the book back in the window - sill, dashed a few crumbs from his jacket, assumed a mortar - board cap that was lying on the tea - caddy, and went forth to his evening"preparation duty. [/]
[Lewisham] "The West Street was empty and shining golden with the sunset. [/]
[Narrator] Its beauty seized upon him, and he forgot to repeat the passage from Henry VIII. [/]
[Narrator] that should have occupied him down the street. [/]
[Narrator] Instead he was presently thinking of that insubordinate glance from his window and of little chins and nose - tips. [/]
[Narrator] His eyes became remote in their expression. .. . [/]
[AnObsequiousLittleBoyWithLinesToBeExamined] The school door was opened by an obsequious little boy with"lines"to be examined. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Lewisham felt a curious change of atmosphere on his entry. [/]
[Narrator] The door slammed behind him. [/]
[Narrator] The hall with its insistent scholastic suggestions, its yellow marbled paper, its long rows of hat - pegs, its disreputable array of umbrellas, a broken mortar - board and a tattered and scattered _ Principia _, seemed dim and dull in contrast with the luminous stir of the early March evening outside. [/]
[Narrator] An unusual sense of the greyness of a teacher's life, of the greyness indeed of the life of all studious souls came, and went in his mind. [/]
[Lewisham] He took the"lines,"written painfully over three pages of exercise book, and obliterated them with a huge G. E. L. , scrawled monstrously across each page. [/]
[Narrator] He heard the familiar mingled noises of the playground drifting in to him through the open schoolroom door. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER II. [/]
[Lewisham] "AS THE WIND BLOWS. [/]
[Lewisham] "A flaw in that pentagram of a time - table, that pentagram by which the demons of distraction were to be excluded from Mr. Lewisham's career to Greatness, was the absence of a clause forbidding study out of doors. [/]
[Narrator] It was the day after the trivial window peeping of the last chapter that this gap in the time - table became apparent, a day if possible more gracious and alluring than its predecessor, and at half - past twelve, instead of returning from the school directly to his lodging, Mr. Lewisham escaped through the omission and made his way -- Horace in pocket -- to the park gates and so to the avenue of ancient trees that encircles the broad Whortley domain. [/]
[Narrator] He dismissed a suspicion of his motive with perfect success. [/]
[Narrator] In the avenue -- for the path is but little frequented -- one might expect to read undisturbed. [/]
[Narrator] The open air, the erect attitude, are surely better than sitting in a stuffy, enervating bedroom. [/]
[Narrator] The open air is distinctly healthy, hardy, simple. .. . [/]
[Narrator] The day was breezy, and there was a perpetual rustling, a going and coming in the budding trees. [/]
[Narrator] The network of the beeches was full of golden sunlight, and all the lower branches were shot with horizontal dashes of new - born green. [/]
[Lewisham] "_ Tu, nisi ventis Debes ludibrium, cave _. [/]
[Lewisham] "was the appropriate matter of Mr. Lewisham's thoughts, and he was mechanically trying to keep the book open in three places at once, at the text, the notes, and the literal translation, while he turned up the vocabulary for _ ludibrium _, when his attention, wandering dangerously near the top of the page, fell over the edge and escaped with incredible swiftness down the avenue. .. . A girl, wearing a straw hat adorned with white blossom, was advancing towards him. [/]
[Narrator] Her occupation, too, was literary. [/]
[Narrator] Indeed, she was so busy writing that evidently she did not perceive him. [/]
[Narrator] Unreasonable emotions descended upon Mr. Lewisham -- emotions that are unaccountable on the mere hypothesis of a casual meeting. [/]
[Lewisham] Something was whispered; it sounded suspiciously like"It's her!" [/]

[Narrator] Her fluttering skirt was short, and showed her shoes and ankles. [/]
[Narrator] He noted her graceful, easy steps. [/]

[Narrator] CHAPTER III. [/]
[Narrator] THE WONDERFUL DISCOVERY. [/]
[Narrator] On Sunday it was Lewisham's duty to accompany the boarders twice to church. [/]
[Narrator] The boys sat in the gallery above the choirs facing the organ loft and at right angles to the general congregation. [/]

[Narrator] In those days much of Lewisham's mind was still an unknown land to him. [/]

[Narrator] Music, for instance, carried him away, and particularly the effect of many voices in unison whirled him off from almost any state of mind to a fine massive emotionality. [/]
[Narrator] And the evening service at Whortley church -- at the evening service surplices were worn -- the chanting and singing, the vague brilliance of the numerous candle flames, the multitudinous unanimity of the congregation down there, kneeling, rising, thunderously responding, invariably inebriated him. [/]
[Narrator] Inspired him, if you will, and turned the prose of his life into poetry. [/]
[Narrator] And Chance, coming to the aid of Dame Nature, dropped just the apt suggestion into his now highly responsive ear. [/]

[Narrator] He felt the blood rushing to his face. [/]
[Narrator] Love! [/]
[Narrator] The greatest of these. [/]
[Narrator] The greatest of all things. [/]
[Narrator] Better than fame. [/]
[Narrator] Better than knowledge. [/]

[Narrator] He, Mr. Lewisham, was in Love. [/]
[Lewisham] "A. .. men." [/]

[Narrator] Lewisham was just then discovering Socialism. [/]
[Narrator] That had afforded a basis of conversation -- an incentive to intercourse. [/]
[Narrator] She seemed to find something rarely interesting in his peculiar view of things, and, as chance would have it, he met her accidentally quite a number of times, in the corridors of the schools, in the big Education Library, and in the Art Museum. [/]

[Narrator] Lewisham for the first time in his life began to fancy he had conversational powers. [/]
[Narrator] She resolved to stir up his ambitions -- an easy task. [/]
[Narrator] She thought he had exceptional gifts and that she might serve to direct them; she certainly developed his vanity. [/]
[Narrator] She had matriculated at the London University and they took the Intermediate Examination in Science together in July -- she a little unwisely -- which served, as almost anything will serve in such cases, as a further link between them. [/]
[Narrator] She failed, which in no way diminished Lewisham's regard for her. [/]
[Narrator] On the examination days they discoursed about Friendship in general, and things like that, down the Burlington Arcade during the lunch time -- Burlington Arcade undisguisedly amused by her learned dinginess and his red tie -- and among other things that were said she reproached him for not reading poetry. [/]
[Narrator] When they parted in Piccadilly, after the examination, they agreed to write, about poetry and themselves, during the holidays, and then she lent him, with a touch of hesitation, Rossetti's poems. [/]

[Narrator] CHAPTER XIV. [/]
[Narrator] MR. LAGUNE'S POINT OF VIEW. [/]
[Narrator] For three days the Laboratory at South Kensington saw nothing of Lagune, and then he came back more invincibly voluble than ever. [/]

[Narrator] CHAPTER XV. [/]
[Narrator] LOVE IN THE STREETS. [/]
[Narrator] Lewisham was not quite clear what course he meant to take in the high enterprise of foiling Lagune, and indeed he was anything but clear about the entire situation. [/]
[Narrator] His logical processes, his emotions and his imagination seemed playing some sort of snatching game with his will. [/]
[Narrator] Enormous things hung imminent, but it worked out to this, that he walked home with Ethel night after night for -- to be exact -- seven - and - sixty nights. [/]
[Narrator] Every week night through November and December, save once, when he had to go into the far East to buy himself an overcoat, he was waiting to walk with her home. [/]
[Narrator] A curious, inconclusive affair, that walk, to which he came nightly full of vague longings, and which ended invariably under an odd shadow of disappointment. [/]
[Narrator] It began outside Lagune's most punctually at five, and ended -- mysteriously -- at the corner of a side road in Clapham, a road of little yellow houses with sunk basements and tawdry decorations of stone. [/]
[Narrator] Up that road she vanished night after night, into a grey mist and the shadow beyond a feeble yellow gas - lamp, and he would watch her vanish, and then sigh and turn back towards his lodgings. [/]
[Narrator] They talked of this and that, their little superficial ideas about themselves, and of their circumstances and tastes, and always there was something, something that was with them unspoken, unacknowledged, which made all these things unreal and insincere. [/]
[Narrator] Yet out of their talk he began to form vague ideas of the home from which she came. [/]
[Narrator] There was, of course, no servant, and the mother was something meandering, furtive, tearful in the face of troubles. [/]
[Narrator] Sometimes of an afternoon or evening she grew garrulous. [/]
[TheMother] "Mother does talk so -- sometimes." [/]
[Narrator] She rarely went out of doors. [/]
[Narrator] Chaffery always rose late, and would sometimes go away for days together. [/]
[Narrator] He was mean; he allowed only a weekly twenty - five shillings for housekeeping, and sometimes things grew unsatisfactory at the week - end. [/]
[Narrator] There seemed to be little sympathy between mother and daughter; the widow had been flighty in a dingy fashion, and her marriage with her chief lodger Chaffery had led to unforgettable sayings. [/]
[Narrator] It was to facilitate this marriage that Ethel had been sent to Whortley, so that was counted a mitigated evil. [/]
[Narrator] But these were far - off things, remote and unreal down the long, ill - lit vista of the suburban street which swallowed up Ethel nightly. [/]
[Narrator] The walk, her warmth and light and motion close to him, her clear little voice, and the touch of her hand; that was reality. [/]
[Narrator] The shadow of Chaffery and his deceptions lay indeed across all these things, sometimes faint, sometimes dark and present. [/]
[Narrator] Then Lewisham became insistent, his sentimental memories ceased, and he asked questions that verged on gulfs of doubt. [/]
[She] Had she ever"helped"? [/]
[Narrator] She had not, she declared. [/]
[She] Then she added that twice at home she had"sat down"to complete the circle. [/]
[Narrator] She would never help again. [/]
[Narrator] That she promised -- if it needed promising. [/]
[Narrator] There had already been dreadful trouble at home about the exposure at Lagune's. [/]
[Narrator] Her mother had sided with her stepfather and joined in blaming her. [/]
[Lewisham] But was she to blame?"Of _ course _ you were not to blame,"said Lewisham. [/]
[Narrator] Lagune, he learnt, had been unhappy and restless for the three days after the _ séance_--indulging in wearisome monologue -- with Ethel as sole auditor (at twenty - one shillings a week). [/]
[Narrator] Then he had decided to give Chaffery a sound lecture on his disastrous dishonesty. [/]
[Narrator] But it was Chaffery gave the lecture. [/]
[Narrator] Smithers, had he only known it, had been overthrown by a better brain than Lagune's, albeit it spoke through Lagune's treble. [/]
[Narrator] Ethel did not like talking of Chaffery and these other things. [/]
[She] "If you knew how sweet it was to forget it all,"she would say;"to be just us two together for a little while." [/]
[She] And,"What good _ does _ it do to keep on?" [/]
[Narrator] when Lewisham was pressing. [/]
[Narrator] Lewisham wanted very much to keep on at times, but the good of it was a little hard to demonstrate. [/]
[Narrator] So his knowledge of the situation remained imperfect and the weeks drifted by. [/]
[Narrator] Wonderfully varied were those seven - and - sixty nights, as he came to remember in after life. [/]
[Narrator] There were nights of damp and drizzle, and then thick fogs, beautiful, isolating, grey - white veils, turning every yard of pavement into a private room. [/]
[Narrator] Grand indeed were these fogs, things to rejoice at mightily, since then it was no longer a thing for public scorn when two young people hurried along arm in arm, and one could do a thousand impudent, significant things with varying pressure and the fondling of a little hand (a hand in a greatly mended glove of cheap kid). [/]
[Narrator] Then indeed one seemed to be nearer that elusive something that threaded it all together. [/]
[Narrator] And the dangers of the street corners, the horses looming up suddenly out of the dark, the carters with lanterns at their horses'heads, the street lamps, blurred, smoky orange at one's nearest, and vanishing at twenty yards into dim haze, seemed to accentuate the infinite need of protection on the part of a delicate young lady who had already traversed three winters of fogs, thornily alone. [/]
[Narrator] Moreover, one could come right down the quiet street where she lived, halfway to the steps of her house, with a delightful sense of enterprise. [/]
[Narrator] The fogs passed all too soon into a hard frost, into nights of starlight and presently moonlight, when the lamps looked hard, flashing like rows of yellow gems, and their reflections and the glare of the shop windows were sharp and frosty, and even the stars hard and bright, snapping noiselessly (if one may say so) instead of twinkling. [/]
[Narrator] A jacket trimmed with imitation Astrachan replaced Ethel's lighter coat, and a round cap of Astrachan her hat, and her eyes shone hard and bright, and her forehead was broad and white beneath it. [/]
[Narrator] It was exhilarating, but one got home too soon, and so the way from Chelsea to Clapham was lengthened, first into a loop of side streets, and then when the first pulverulent snows told that Christmas was at hand, into a new loop down King's Road, and once even through the Brompton Road and Sloane Street, where the shops were full of decorations and entertaining things. [/]
[Narrator] And, under circumstances of infinite gravity, Mr. Lewisham secretly spent three - and - twenty shillings out of the vestiges of that hundred pounds, and bought Ethel a little gold ring set with pearls. [/]
[Narrator] With that there must needs be a ceremonial, and on the verge of the snowy, foggy Common she took off her glove and the ring was placed on her finger. [/]
[Narrator] Whereupon he was moved to kiss her -- on the frost - pink knuckle next to an inky nail. [/]
[ADelicateYoungLadyWhoHadAlreadyTraversedThreeWintersOfFogsThornilyAlone] "It's silly of us,"she said. [/]

[Narrator] Afterwards he thought over those promises, and another evening went into the matter more fully, telling her of all the brilliant things that he held it was possible for a South Kensington student to do and be -- of headmasterships, northern science schools, inspectorships, demonstratorships, yea, even professorships. [/]
[Narrator] And then, and then -- To all of which she lent a willing and incredulous ear, finding in that dreaming a quality of fear as well as delight. [/]
[ADelicateYoungLadyWhoHadAlreadyTraversedThreeWintersOfFogsThornilyAlone] The putting on of the pearl - set ring was mere ceremonial, of course; she could not wear it either at Lagune's or at home, so instead she threaded it on a little white satin ribbon and wore it round her neck--"next her heart." [/]
[ADelicateYoungLadyWhoHadAlreadyTraversedThreeWintersOfFogsThornilyAlone] He thought of it there warm"next her heart."When he had bought the ring he had meant to save it for Christmas before he gave it to her. [/]
[Narrator] But the desire to see her pleasure had been too strong for him. [/]
[Narrator] Christmas Eve, I know not by what deceit on her part, these young people spent together all day. [/]
[Narrator] Lagune was down with a touch of bronchitis and had given his typewriter a holiday. [/]
[Narrator] Perhaps she forgot to mention it at home. [/]
[Narrator] The Royal College was in vacation and Lewisham was free. [/]
[Lewisham] He declined the plumber's invitation;"work"kept him in London, he said, though it meant a pound or more of added expenditure. [/]
[Narrator] These absurd young people walked sixteen miles that Christmas Eve, and parted warm and glowing. [/]
[Narrator] There had been a hard frost and a little snow, the sky was a colourless grey, icicles hung from the arms of the street lamps, and the pavements were patterned out with frond - like forms that were trodden into slides as the day grew older. [/]
[Narrator] The Thames they knew was a wonderful sight, but that they kept until last. [/]
[Narrator] They went first along the Brompton Road. .. . [/]
[Narrator] And it is well that you should have the picture of them right: Lewisham in the ready - made overcoat, blue cloth and velvet collar, dirty tan gloves, red tie, and bowler hat; and Ethel in a two - year - old jacket and hat of curly Astrachan; both pink - cheeked from the keen air, shyly arm in arm occasionally, and very alert to miss no possible spectacle. [/]
[Narrator] The shops were varied and interesting along the Brompton Road, but nothing to compare with Piccadilly. [/]
[Narrator] There were windows in Piccadilly so full of costly little things, it took fifteen minutes to get them done, card shops, drapers'shops full of foolish, entertaining attractions. [/]
[Narrator] Lewisham, in spite of his old animosities, forgot to be severe on the Shopping Class, Ethel was so vastly entertained by all these pretty follies. [/]

[Narrator] But indeed the Thames was a wonderful sight that year! [/]

[Narrator] He was trying to see the situation clearly. [/]
[Narrator] As he was just smarting acutely under his defeat, this speaks little for the clearness of his mind. [/]

[Narrator] The rich prettiness of his love - making had fled to some remote quarter of his being. [/]

[Narrator] CHAPTER XX. [/]
[Narrator] THE CAREER IS SUSPENDED. [/]
[Narrator] On the Wednesday afternoon following this -- it was hard upon the botanical examination -- Mr. Lewisham was observed by Smithers in the big Education Library reading in a volume of the British Encyclopaedia. [/]
[Narrator] Beside him were the current Whitaker's Almanac, an open note - book, a book from the Contemporary Science Series, and the Science and Art Department's Directory. [/]
[Narrator] Smithers, who had a profound sense of Lewisham's superiority in the art of obtaining facts of value in examinations, wondered for some minutes what valuable tip for a student in botany might be hidden in Whitaker, and on reaching his lodgings spent some time over the landlady's copy. [/]

[Chaffery] Think of the chubby, comfortable creature! [/]

[Chaffery] Imagine him bullying his gardener over some transplanted geraniums, the thick mist of lies they stand in, so that the man does not immediately with the edge of a spade smite down his impertinence to the dust from which it rose. .. . [/]
[Chaffery] And his case is the case of all comfortable lives. [/]

[/]

[Narrator] THE GLAMOUR FADES. [/]
[Narrator] After all, the rosy love - making and marrying and Epithalamy are no more than the dawn of things, and to follow comes all the spacious interval of white laborious light. [/]
[Narrator] Try as we may to stay those delightful moments, they fade and pass remorselessly; there is no returning, no recovering, only -- for the foolish -- the vilest peep - shows and imitations in dens and darkened rooms. [/]
[Narrator] We go on -- we grow. [/]
[Narrator] At least we age. [/]
[Narrator] Our young couple, emerging presently from an atmosphere of dusk and morning stars, found the sky gathering greyly overhead and saw one another for the first time clearly in the light of every - day. [/]
[Narrator] It might perhaps witness better to Lewisham's refinement if one could tell only of a moderated and dignified cooling, of pathetic little concealments of disappointment and a decent maintenance of the sentimental atmosphere. [/]
[Narrator] And so at last daylight. [/]
[Narrator] But our young couple were too crude for that. [/]
[Narrator] The first intimations of their lack of identity have already been described, but it would be tedious and pitiful to tell of all the little intensifications, shade by shade, of the conflict of their individualities. [/]
[Narrator] They fell out, dear lady! [/]
[Narrator] they came to conflict of words. [/]
[Narrator] The stress of perpetual worry was upon them, of dwindling funds and the anxious search for work that would not come. [/]
[Narrator] And on Ethel lay long, vacant, lonely hours in dull surroundings. [/]
[Narrator] Differences arose from the most indifferent things; one night Lewisham lay awake in unfathomable amazement because she had convinced him she did not care a rap for the Welfare of Humanity, and deemed his Socialism a fancy and an indiscretion. [/]
[Narrator] And one Sunday afternoon they started for a walk under the pleasantest auspices, and returned flushed and angry, satire and retort flying free -- on the score of the social conventions in Ethel's novelettes. [/]
[Narrator] For some inexplicable reason Lewisham saw fit to hate her novelettes very bitterly. [/]

[Narrator] And always each skirmish left its scar, effaced from yet another line of their lives the lingering tints of romantic colour. [/]
[Narrator] There came no work, no added income for either of them, saving two trifles, for five long months. [/]
[Narrator] Once Lewisham won twelve shillings in the prize competition of a penny weekly, and three times came infinitesimal portions of typewriting from a poet who had apparently seen the _ Athenaeum _ advertisement. [/]
[Narrator] His name was Edwin Peak Baynes and his handwriting was sprawling and unformed. [/]

[Narrator] Both of our young people were greatly exercised by these instructions. [/]
[Narrator] One fragment was called"Bird Song,"one"Cloud Shadows,"and one"Eryngium,"but Lewisham thought they might be spoken of collectively as Bosh. [/]
[Narrator] By way of payment, this poet sent, in contravention of the postal regulations, half a sovereign stuck into a card, asking her to keep the balance against future occasions. [/]

[/]
[Narrator] They both felt that the youthfulness of Edwin Peak Baynes detracted something from the reality of this employment. [/]
[Narrator] From his marriage until the final examination in June, Lewisham's life had an odd amphibious quality. [/]
[Narrator] At home were Ethel and the perpetual aching pursuit of employment, the pelting irritations of Madam Gadow's persistent overcharges, and so forth, and amid such things he felt extraordinarily grown up; but intercalated with these experiences were those intervals at Kensington, scraps of his adolescence, as it were, lying amidst the new matter of his manhood, intervals during which he was simply an insubordinate and disappointing student with an increasing disposition to gossip. [/]
[Narrator] At South Kensington he dwelt with theories and ideals as a student should; at the little rooms in Chelsea -- they grew very stuffy as the summer came on, and the accumulation of the penny novelettes Ethel favoured made a litter -- there was his particular private concrete situation, and ideals gave place to the real. [/]
[Narrator] It was a strangely narrow world, he perceived dimly, in which his manhood opened. [/]
[Narrator] The only visitors were the Chafferys. [/]

[Narrator] CHAPTER XXVII. CONCERNING A QUARREL. [/]
[Narrator] It was late in September that this particular quarrel occurred. [/]

[Narrator] On Tuesday Lewisham returned from Vigours'at five -- at half - past six he would go on to his science class at Walham Green -- and discovered Mrs. Chaffery and Ethel in tears. [/]
